_source line 10269 · 2026-05-20T15:18:55.560059+00:00_

15	\paragraph{Probing Methodology and Layer Selection.}
16	Causal abstraction~\cite{geiger2025causal} formalizes when high-level causal models faithfully describe neural computation, grounding use of internal representations. Linear probing spans layer-wise classifiers~\cite{alain2017understanding}, sentence probing~\cite{conneau2018probing}, and surveys~\cite{belinkov2019analysis,belinkov2022probing,rogers2020primer}. Control tasks~\cite{hewitt2019control} distinguish structure from memorization (we adopt these; \cref{app:control}). Information-theoretic and causal formulations address probe complexity~\cite{voita2020information,pimentel2020information,ravfogel2020null,ravfogel2022rlace,belrose2023leace,elazar2021amnesic}. \citet{marks2024geometry} find emergent linear structure in LLM truth representations, consistent with our safety commitment separability. That information distributes non-uniformly across layers is well established~\cite{tenney2019bert,jawahar2019bert}. Cross-layer prediction consistency has been exploited for efficiency: \citet{kaya2019shallow} identify ``overthinking'' via cross-layer disagreement, and \citet{zhou2020bert} formalize patience-based early exit. We repurpose this for safety probe layer selection (\cref{sec:layer-sensitivity}). Our contribution is orthogonal: the known layer-wise information gradient interacts with metric choice to produce systematic selection bias in safety probes. Most directly, \citet{boxo2025linear_probes} show linear probes can rely on textual evidence rather than deeper representations; our metric bias analysis provides a complementary mechanism: shallow-layer probes exploit compressed representations that inflate crossing rate, a geometric effect consistent with text-leakage at layers where representations have not yet diverged from surface features. \citet{alvarez2026transport_geometry} apply hidden-state geometry to step-level hallucination detection concurrently with our work. Despite this foundation, la

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has received almost no scrutiny. We show this choice alone can reverse a study's central conclusion---the same model and data yield a 14-layer (R1-8B) to 61-layer 
…[truncated 3273 chars]

_source line 10325 · 2026-05-20T16:32:10.377174+00:00_

1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against a 384-dimensional distilled sentence encoder (MiniLM-L6-v2; Holm-Bonferroni corrected; upper bound); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are cross-validated (nested CV): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\dagger$ ($^\dagger$\,sample-limited, $n \leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-32B shows a pattern consistent with position mediation and falls below minimum detectable effect. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \leq 0.002$). Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control.
2

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Recent work shows that chain-of-thought faithfulness is limited~\citep{chen2025unfaithful}, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses surface text. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides retrospective classification (not online detection) of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity has rece

1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition. This binary formulation is a deliberate simplification; we discuss its limits in \cref{sec:limitations}. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The classification task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10	
11	\begin{table}[t]
12	\centering
13	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
14	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
15	\toprule
16	\textbf{Model} & \textbf{Arch.} & \textbf{Params} & \textbf{Layers} & \textbf{Hidden} & \textbf{$n$ (total\,/\,test)} \\
17	\midrule
18	R1-8B & Llama & 8B & 32 & 4096 & 209\,/\,43 \\
19	OT-7B & Qwen & 7B & 28 & 3584 & 228\,/\,47 \\
20	R1-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 100\,/\,20 \\
21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} We classify HS advantage evidence into three tiers: \emph{cross-validated}\footnote{In our usage, \emph{cross-validated} means the result survives nested 5-fold cross-validation with a held-out test fold, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. \emph{Exploratory} ($\dagger$) results pass HB on the holdout split but lack nested CV validation due to sample constraints.} (nested CV pass; 7--8B models), \emph{significant} (HB pass without nested CV; QwQ-32B), and \emph{exploratory} ($^\dagger$, small sample or narrow coverage; R1-32B). Per-model details follow in \cref{sec:layer-results,sec:hs-text,sec:32b-analysis}.
6	
7	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Classification requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.\looseness=-1
10	
11	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
12	
13	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the crossing-rate composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers across all four models: L0 for R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) and OT-7B
…[truncated 13829 chars]

We adopt a two-stage design: upstream exploration of layers and windows is protected by nested CV and split-half validation for 7--8B models (\\cref{app:nested_cv,app:split_half}); downstream modality comparisons apply HB correction to 12 final tests (3 metrics $\\times$ 4 models). The 32B models lack nested CV safeguards (each fold would contain ${\\sim}$4 traces per class) and are accordingly marked exploratory ($\\dagger$)."
}

  "new_string": "R1-32B suggests commitment 11--13 steps early (+14.2pp over position baseline) but covers only 25\\% of traces ($W{=}1$); the precision effect (+15.4pp, CI width 13.9pp) falls below MDE at 80\\% power. QwQ-32B achieves significance at all window sizes with 74\\% coverage but negative HS lead time; the precision effect (+19.2pp, CI width 24.2pp) exceeds MDE (8.6--9.5pp; \\cref{app:power_analysis}) but the position baseline achieves higher BA (0.832 vs.\\ 0.796), and the HS precision margin over position (+4.5pp, $p{=}0.133$) is not significant at $n{=}19$. The divergence suggests per-model validation is needed."
}

### Tool result

_source line 10361 · 2026-05-20T16:35:52.408379+00:00_

11	Our contributions are:
12	\begin{itemize}
13	    \item \textbf{Metric-induced layer selection bias.} We identify a metric-induced systematic bias: crossing-rate composite metrics systematically select shallow layers across all four models (including Llama), reversing HS probe conclusions at 32B scale; a random-label control confirms the bias is geometric (\cref{sec:layer-results}).
14	
15	    \item \textbf{Doubly conditional HS advantage.} Against 384d MiniLM, the HS advantage is validated via nested 5-fold CV at 7--8B (+20.1 to +21.9pp, HB corrected), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp). This advantage is doubly conditional: it diminishes with stronger text encoders (1/4 HB under BGE-1024d; PCA capacity matching preserves significance) and partially overlaps with temporal position (3/4 retain non-positional significance under linear residualization; 2/4 under quadratic control; \cref{sec:32b-analysis}).
16	
17	    \item \textbf{Negative results define method boundaries.} No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing at any evidence tier (\cref{tab:negative}): BOCPD/CUSUM fail on gradual transitions; Mahalanobis distance finds near-zero separation; RepE shows inconsistent performance across models (competitive on QwQ-32B, poor elsewhere); cross-model transfer fails under PCA alignment. Control task selectivity (11.1--16.8pp above chance; \cref{app:control}) confirms genuine safety structure.
18	\end{itemize}
19	
20	\noindent These findings provide initial characterization on one benchmark for hidden-state safety monitoring and identify layer selection as a critical confound that future work must address.\looseness=-1

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry, cross-dataset (AdvBench volatility ratio $1.67\times$; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}), and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2023causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-po
…[truncated 706 chars]

1	\section{Experiments}\label{sec:experiments}
2	
3	We evaluate temporal probes across four reasoning models (\cref{tab:models}), comparing hidden-state (HS) and text modalities under different layer selection criteria.
4	
5	\paragraph{Evidence gradient.} We classify HS advantage evidence into three tiers: \emph{cross-validated}\footnote{In our usage, \emph{cross-validated} means the result survives nested 5-fold cross-validation with a held-out test fold, ruling out hyperparameter overfitting. \emph{Exploratory} ($\dagger$) results pass HB on the holdout split but lack nested CV validation due to sample constraints.} (nested CV pass; 7--8B models), \emph{significant} (HB pass without nested CV; QwQ-32B), and \emph{exploratory} ($^\dagger$, small sample or narrow coverage; R1-32B). Per-model details follow in \cref{sec:layer-results,sec:hs-text,sec:32b-analysis}.
6	
7	\subsection{Setup}\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	We study R1-8B (Llama-8B, R1-distilled, $n{=}43$ test traces), OT-7B (OpenThinker-7B, trained on the OpenThoughts dataset~\citep{openthinker}; Qwen-7B backbone, STILL-trained, $n{=}47$), QwQ-32B (Qwen-32B, native reasoning, $n{=}19$--$20$), and R1-32B (Qwen-32B, R1-distilled, $n{=}20$). All traces come from HarmThoughts with step-level CP annotations (60/20/20 split). HS probes extract last-token hidden states from a selected layer; text probes encode each step with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 (384d). Both use L2-regularized logistic regression with temporal windowing $W \in \{1, 3, 5, 10, 15, 20, 25\}$ selected on validation. Classification requires $K{=}5$ consecutive threshold crossings. Statistical testing follows \cref{sec:evaluation}.\looseness=-1
10	
11	\subsection{Layer Selection Results}\label{sec:layer-results}
12	
13	\cref{tab:layer_selection} shows that the crossing-rate composite metric (balanced accuracy $\times$ crossing rate) systematically selects shallow layers across all four models: L0 for R1-8B (full L0--L24 sweep; \cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}) and OT-7B
…[truncated 13759 chars]

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	Layer selection is a researcher degree of freedom that can reverse conclusions; the multi-metric audit protocol (\cref{sec:discussion}) mitigates this. Contribution~1 generalizes geometrically; Contribution~2 (+15--22pp precision) is cross-validated at 7--8B, exploratory at 32B$^\dagger$, and encoder-conditional; seven alternative methods fail (\cref{tab:negative}).\looseness=-1
4

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Recent work shows that chain-of-thought faithfulness is limited~\citep{chen2025unfaithful}, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses surface text. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides \emph{retrospective} classification---not online detection---of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity
…[truncated 3516 chars]

  "old_string": "A multiplicative composite (BA $\\times$ crossing rate) serves as the \\emph{crossing-rate composite} (CR-composite) baseline, so named because it incorporates crossing rate in the selection criterion. Metrics evaluating probe performance at a fixed decision threshold (precision, inverse FPR, additive composite) form the \\emph{threshold-based} selection. This classification is an empirical observation from metric disagreement, not an a priori design choice; however, given the geometric explanation (cosine compression inflates noise-floor crossings), we predict a priori that any threshold-crossing metric would exhibit shallow-layer bias---a prediction the empirical taxonomy confirms. Crossing rate inflates at shallow layers where representations are compressed (inter-step cosine distances 1.4--2.7$\\times$ smaller; \\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), an independently verifiable geometric property. Precision, inverse FPR, and additive composite all derive from the same $2\\times2$ confusion matrix and empirically converge to the same layer, consistent with their shared structure but not mathematically guaranteed; BA is threshold-free and provides independent confirmation. The ablation diagnoses which \\emph{aspect} drives layer selection bias. The effective number of independent tests is four (one per model), reflected in our HB correction. Results in \\cref{sec:layer-results}; full per-layer sweeps in \\cref{app:layer_sweep}.\\looseness=-1",

Threshold-based metrics share a confusion matrix and empirically converge; BA independently confirms. _source line 10407 · 2026-05-20T16:42:54.029160+00:00_

1	\section{Related Work}\label{sec:related-work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Safety in Reasoning Models.}
4	Reasoning models introduce a qualitatively new failure mode: the model's own chain of thought can transition from safety-aware deliberation to harmful generation without adversarial input modification~\cite{wang2025lrmsafety}.
5	
6	\textit{Attack characterization.} Self-jailbreak has been documented with step-level annotations~\cite{harmthoughts}, with longer chains increasing circumvention probability~\cite{selfjailbreak}. H-CoT~\cite{hcot2025} shows reasoning chains can be externally hijacked. Unfaithful reasoning~\cite{chen2025unfaithful,turpin2023language} further motivates hidden-state monitoring that bypasses text. Safety-aligned models maintain refusal intentions during thinking but experience a sharp drop at output generation~\cite{yin2025refusal_cliff,hagendorff2026lrm_jailbreak,hu2026refusal_dynamics,ahrend2026safer_traces}.
7	
8	\textit{Detection.} CoT monitoring can detect reward hacking but optimization pressure promotes obfuscation~\cite{baker2025monitoring}. CoT monitorability is valuable but fragile~\cite{korbak2025cot_monitorability}. GuardReasoner~\cite{xiang2025guardreasoner} trains a dedicated reasoning-based guard model. \citet{schulz2025roadmap} proposes a structured safety-case framework grounded in CoT monitoring, formalizing the deployment context our probes would serve.
9	
10	\textit{Mitigation.} SafeThink~\cite{safethink} steers early reasoning toward safety, SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} inserts entropy-triggered reminders, and Chain-of-Guardrails~\cite{mao2025selfjailbreak_reasoning} proposes guardrail insertion during reasoning. None of these conducts a systematic modality comparison for commitment detection.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hidden-State Safety Detection.}
13	Internal activations can detect safety violations in standard LLMs. \citet{azaria2023internal} demonstrate that internal states encode whether a model's output is truthful, motivating r
…[truncated 6222 chars]

    "old_string": "\\citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal layer representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts; their results confirm that layer-wise structure carries safety-relevant signal. Concurrently, \\citet{chan2025probing} probe safety commitment in LLMs using linear classifiers on internal representations. Their work validates that commitment transitions are linearly separable in hidden-state space, but does not investigate how the choice of evaluation metric or transformer layer affects probe conclusions. Our work complements theirs by showing that this separability is layer-dependent and that metric choice can reverse the apparent modality advantage---a methodological concern relevant to any hidden-state safety probe.",
    "new_string": "\\citet{kadali2025internal} decompose internal representations to separate jailbreak from benign prompts, confirming layer-wise safety signal. Concurrently, \\citet{chan2025probing} validate that commitment transitions are linearly separable but do not investigate layer or metric sensitivity; our work shows separability is layer-dependent and metric choice can reverse the modality advantage."
  }
}

64	
65	The HS advantage is encoder-conditional: against BGE-large (1024d), only OT-7B survives HB correction (\cref{app:encoder_robustness}). Multi-seed stability appears in \cref{app:multi_seed}. Against the 384d baseline, HS probes outperform text probes in all four models$^\dagger$ (\cref{tab:hs_vs_text}): cross-validated at 7--8B (OT-7B: +21.9pp precision, R1-8B: +20.1pp), significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness at QwQ-32B$^\dagger$ (+19.2pp), and exploratory at R1-32B$^\dagger$ (+15.4pp, pattern consistent with position mediation). R1-8B FPR is directionally consistent (5/5 seeds) but not robust ($p{=}0.044$, rank 12/12 in HB step-down; 1/5 seeds Bonferroni; \cref{app:multi_seed}). R1-32B passes 5/5 seeds but shows a pattern consistent with position mediation (residualized gap NS; \cref{app:position_residual}); QwQ-32B's position baseline is competitive (\cref{sec:32b-analysis}). Overall, 18/20 model-seed combinations pass full HB (\cref{app:multi_seed}). Control task selectivity confirms genuine safety signal: 11.1--16.8pp above chance (\cref{app:control}). A split-half analysis on OT-7B confirms internal validity (\cref{app:split_half}).\looseness=-1
66	
67	\paragraph{Capacity control.} PCA reduction of HS features to 384d (79--92\% variance retained) preserves the precision advantage across all four models$^\dagger$ (+16.8 to +23.1pp, all $p{<}0.012$, all HB). The control provides partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Random projection to 384d yields similar results ($\Delta$Prec +8.3 to +20.5pp; \cref{app:random_projection}), further weakening the capacity explanation. E5-Mistral-7B (4096d$\rightarrow$PCA 384d) yields intermediate results (2/4 HB; \cref{app:e5_mistral}), forming a monotonic encoder-quality gradient with MiniLM (4/4) and BGE-large (1/4).\looseness=-1
68

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry, cross-dataset (AdvBench volatility ratio $1.67\times$; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}), and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp), partial evidence against a pure capacity explanation. Whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2023causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-po

108	CP-aligned probe score trajectories (\cref{fig:trajectory} in Appendix) confirm sharper HS transitions at the commitment point, with OT-7B showing the clearest separation and R1-32B the weakest.\looseness=-1
109	
110	\enlargethispage{4\baselineskip}
111	\subsection{Negative Results}\label{sec:negative}
112	
113	No alternative method consistently matches supervised probing (\cref{tab:negative}). BOCPD~\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail (0\% detection on 3/4 models; \cref{app:bocpd_config}); Mahalanobis distance yields near-zero separation; RepE~\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (QwQ-32B BA 0.756 vs.\ R1-32B 0.559); MLP probes overfit; CCS misaligns with temporal structure; cross-model transfer fails. These failures define four requirements: the signal is directional, gradual, model-specific, and linearly separable. Supervised linear probes with principled layer selection form the minimal effective methodology (\cref{app:negative_details}).\looseness=-1
114

BOCPD~\\citep{adams2007bayesian}/CUSUM fail (0\\% on 3/4 models); Mahalanobis yields near-zero separation; RepE~\\citep{zou2023representation} is model-dependent (BA 0.756 vs.\\ 0.559); MLP probes overfit; CCS misaligns temporally; cross-model transfer fails. These failures show the signal is directional, gradual, model-specific, and linearly separable (\\cref{app:negative_details}).\\looseness=-1"
  }
}

-Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against a 384-dimensional distilled sentence encoder (MiniLM-L6-v2; Holm-Bonferroni corrected; upper bound); this advantage diminishes with stronger encoders (384d: 4/4 models pass HB $\to$ 1024d BGE-large: 1/4). The 7--8B results are cross-validated (nested CV): precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory$^\dagger$ ($^\dagger$\,sample-limited, $n \leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-

_source line 10477 · 2026-05-20T18:06:59.693555+00:00_

1	Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. Detecting the commitment point (CP)---the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases---requires monitoring internal representations or generated text. We study four reasoning LLMs, primarily Qwen-based (3/4 models) with one Llama model for limited cross-architecture evidence, on HarmThoughts and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\ text probe capabilities on this benchmark. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against a 384-dimensional distilled sentence encoder (MiniLM-L6-v2; Holm-Bonferroni corrected; upper bound), but this advantage is doubly conditional: it diminishes with stronger text encoders (4/4 HB at 384d $\to$ 1/4 at 1024d BGE-large) and partially overlaps with temporal position (3/4 models retain significant non-positional advantage under linear residualization; 2/4 under quadratic control). The 7--8B results are validated via nested 5-fold CV: precision and balanced accuracy confirmed; R1-8B safe-trace false positive rate (FPR) is significant but seed-sensitive (3/5 seeds pass HB). The 32B results are exploratory (sample-limited, $n \leq 20$): QwQ-32B is significant but suggestive of position-competitiveness; R1-32B shows a pattern consistent with position mediation and falls below minimum detectable effect. A three-layer ensemble provides metric-agnostic HS advantage matching the best individual metric (4/4 models, $p \leq 0.002$). Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on e

  "new_string": "Reasoning models with chain-of-thought (CoT) can self-jailbreak: their reasoning traces transition from safe deliberation to committed harmful generation without adversarial input. We define the commitment point (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases, and provide initial characterization of hidden-state (HS) vs.\\ text probes for CP detection across four reasoning LLMs on HarmThoughts. The comparison depends critically on which transformer layer is monitored: a crossing-rate composite metric masks the HS advantage at 32B scale by selecting shallow layers where representation compression inflates threshold crossings. Threshold-based layer selection recovers HS precision gains of +15.4 to +21.9pp against a 384d distilled sentence encoder (Holm-Bonferroni corrected; upper bound), but this advantage is doubly conditional: it diminishes with stronger text encoders (4/4 HB at 384d $\\to$ 1/4 at 1024d) and partially overlaps with temporal position. The 7--8B results are validated via nested 5-fold CV; the 32B results ($n \\leq 20$) are exploratory. Seven alternative methods (change-point detection, geometric distance, representation engineering, cross-model transfer) all fail to match supervised probing. Metric-induced layer selection bias is the robust methodological finding; the empirical HS advantage is conditional on encoder quality, model scale, and position control."

1	\section{Problem Setup and Methods}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Safety Commitment Point}
5	\label{sec:commitment-point}
6	
7	When a reasoning model is jailbroken, its chain-of-thought (CoT) transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation. We define the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP) as the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases; traces with oscillating safety deliberation are resolved by taking the final irreversible transition. This binary formulation is a deliberate simplification; we discuss its limits in \cref{sec:limitations}. Steps before CP are \emph{pre-commitment} ($y_t = 0$); steps at or after CP are \emph{post-commitment} ($y_t = 1$). The classification task produces a score $s_t \in [0, 1]$ estimating whether the model has passed its CP by step $t$.
8	
9	We study four reasoning models spanning two architectures and two scales (\cref{tab:models}).
10

21	QwQ-32B & Qwen & 32B & 64 & 5120 & 95\,/\,19 \\
22	\bottomrule
23	\end{tabular}}
24	\caption{Models studied. R1-8B and R1-32B are distilled from DeepSeek-R1~\citep{deepseekr1}; QwQ-32B~\citep{qwq} is a native reasoning model; OT-7B~\citep{openthinker} is trained with STILL~\citep{still} on Qwen2.5-7B. Data: HarmThoughts~\citep{harmthoughts} with step-level CP annotations. Split: 60/20/20.}
25	\label{tab:models}
26	\end{table}

1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Reasoning models such as DeepSeek-R1~\cite{deepseekr1} and QwQ~\cite{qwq} scale inference-time computation through extended chains of thought (CoT). Recent work documents that these models can ``self-jailbreak'': without adversarial input modification, the CoT transitions from safety-aware deliberation to committed harmful generation~\cite{harmthoughts,selfjailbreak}. We define the first reasoning step after which safety deliberation permanently ceases as the \textbf{Safety Commitment Point} (CP). This failure mode differs qualitatively from adversarial jailbreaks on standard LLMs because the harmful transition originates within the model's own reasoning. Recent work shows that chain-of-thought faithfulness is limited~\citep{chen2025unfaithful}, motivating hidden-state monitoring that bypasses surface text. Before retrospective characterization can inform future detection systems, a prior question must be answered: can current probing methods reliably distinguish pre-CP from post-CP reasoning steps, and under what conditions? This paper provides \emph{retrospective} classification---not online detection---of what hidden-state and text-based probes can and cannot detect; false positive rates remain 11--50\%, so deployment applications are future work.\looseness=-1
4	
5	Existing safety detectors target fundamentally different problems. Hidden-state (HS) methods such as TrajGuard~\cite{trajguard} and SafeSwitch~\cite{safeswitch} classify adversarial inputs at the token level, while text monitors like SafeRemind~\cite{saferemind} scan generated reasoning for explicit violations. Neither tracks the gradual, multi-step commitment process. Before asking which modality is better, a prior methodological question must be answered: how sensitive are probe conclusions to the choice of layer selection metric? HS probes require choosing which transformer layer to monitor, a researcher degree of freedom whose metric sensitivity

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	\paragraph{Layer selection as researcher degree of freedom~\citep{gelman2013garden}.}
4	Our five-metric ablation (\cref{sec:layer-results}) shows crossing rate reverses the central finding via shallow-layer compression (\cref{app:cosine_volatility}), architecture-independent across Llama/Qwen (\cref{tab:r18b_full_layer}). A random-label control on QwQ-32B reveals crossing rate retains its shallow preference in 100\% of random-label permutations (CR-composite: 80\%), while precision's deep preference dissolves (only 20\% shallow, scattered across layers; \cref{app:random_cp}): the crossing-rate shallow preference is geometric (persisting under random labels), while threshold-based deep selection requires genuine safety labels. Contribution~1 (layer bias) thus generalizes from geometry, cross-dataset (AdvBench volatility ratio $1.67\times$; \cref{app:cosine_volatility}), and is internally valid (split-half replication; \cref{app:split_half}); Contribution~2 (HS advantage) is sample-dependent. Threshold-based layers peak at L63/64~\citep{wollschlager2025geometry}; R1-32B selectivity is 3.0pp (\cref{app:control}), below the 5pp threshold~\citep{hewitt2019control}.\looseness=-1
5	
6	\paragraph{Encoder-quality boundary.}
7	The HS advantage is significant against 384d MiniLM (cross-validated at 7--8B; exploratory at 32B) but attenuated against 1024d BGE-large (1/4 HB). PCA capacity matching preserves significance (+16.8 to +23.1pp); whether stronger encoders~\citep{wang2024e5mistral,boxo2025linear_probes} close the gap bounds these claims' shelf life.\looseness=-1
8	
9	\paragraph{Guidance for future research.}
10	Converging evidence supports genuine safety representations, but causal validation via DAS~\citep{geiger2023causal}, ITI~\citep{li2024iti}, or circuit breakers~\citep{zou2024improving} is needed. Under linear residualization, 3/4 models retain significant non-positional HS advantage; under quadratic control, only 2
